    One stuck row was writing 80 defect rows a day — the feedback inbox had become 98% noiseFIX

    The Sweep Feedback backlog was not a backlog. The Fixer Ledger carried it as “191 rows at New, needs a triage strategy”. The live count was 494, and 280 of them were byte-identical: Loop rewrite — (step not recorded), arriving at roughly 80 a day since 08-16. In the three days that produced 250 rows, five were real feedback. Nobody was ever going to triage that, which is precisely how a real defect hides.

    One row, one missing mark. The Loop’s REVIEW-absorption beat (services/event-gate/loop.py) files an SOP-improvement row whenever a Next Action’s text no longer matches its Original proposal — the reviewer’s edit is the feedback. But nothing marked the rewrite as absorbed, so the condition stayed true forever. Next Action 3b97503281c48167… (“One Artist card per human”) went Ready with Rewritten checked on 2026-08-11 and never closed, so every tick of the 10-minute desk-loop filed the same row again. Its Source SOP step was blank, which is why all 280 carry the same (step not recorded) title.

    The fix is that filing now ends the condition it reports on. After a successful file, file_sop_improvement() squares Original proposal to the current text and clears the Rewritten checkbox — the diff goes quiet, which is what “this feedback has been captured” actually means. A rewrite files exactly once (dc-canon e3bbce8b). The generating row was also squared by hand, so the bleeding stopped immediately rather than waiting on the next event-gate deploy — verified: no row filed after 21:50Z. The duplicates were closed to Applied with a note naming the cause; the oldest was deliberately left at New, because underneath the noise there is one genuine piece of reviewer feedback and closing all 280 would have thrown it away with the litter.

    The class, which is the part worth keeping. An unattended writer whose trigger condition it does not clear will write forever, and the surface it floods is a queue humans read. Both halves matter: the volume is only the symptom, the real damage is that the 5 real rows became unfindable. Any agent that files into a shared inbox on a repeating tick needs an idempotency mark, not a rate limit.

    Also this pass: alarm-mcp got the deploy.sh treatment flagged as an open failure mode in the 20:45Z entry below — build one immutable tag, block on the build, deploy, then verify the live service is serving that exact tag and exit non-zero if not (danceannex-alarm-monitor 80c7981). It keeps distinct tags rather than :latest, since rollback here is redeploying the previous tag. This service has no version endpoint, so that verify is the only answer to “is my code actually running?”. Not yet run — agent gcloud is classifier-blocked; Junyan runs it.

    Skedda went blind for two days — the session pipe now validates on data, deploys as one unit, and has a canon runbookCHANGE

    The desk read $0 and nobody’s name on every booking, and no error said so. The fleet’s shared Skedda cookie died ~Aug 16 and reads silently degraded to the anonymous public schedule: price 0, payment_status 0, holder null on every row. Writes failed more honestly — The Loop retried one booking move 5 times across two days with “401 — cookie likely expired” before tonight’s fix let it through. Payment verification, the Bookkeeper and the Timekeeper were consuming wrong money data the whole window.

    Three stacked causes, each now fixed. (1) Skedda keeps ONE active session per user — any login as the robot account (a human in a browser, a test run of the refresh job) kills the fleet’s cookie instantly. A login is never a read-only act. (2) The refresh job’s admin-check probed named endpoints, and both choices broke on Skedda’s side, not the cookie’s: /bookingrequestslists answers 422 (anti-forgery — the X-Skedda-RequestVerificationToken header is required on every call, so a bare probe 422s on a perfect cookie) and /venueuserslists now 404s outright. A guard that rejects good cookies after its own login has killed the live one = guaranteed blindness. (3) The job was pinned to a stale image (diag-0818) while rebuilds went to :latest — three probe fixes “shipped” without ever running.

    What shipped. The probe now validates on data: read a 14-day booking window through SkeddaClient and require one priced-or-named booking — precisely what an anonymous session cannot see, immune to endpoint churn (skedda-cli fc06d55). deploy.sh is the only sanctioned deploy path: build once, update the skedda-mcp service AND the skedda-refresh job together, then print both consumers’ images for eyeball proof. The full loop — login → data-probe → store → bounce — proven end to end (execution skedda-refresh-2l5zs, cookie v38, priced read verified after the bounce). Nightly at 10:03Z now runs exactly this path.

    Canonised so Codex and every future session can run it: library/skedda-session.md in dc-canon (63b657af, Notion mirror created with the file per the JOB 35 lesson) — the one-login rule, the blindness signature, the 6-step recovery runbook, the 422-means-anti-forgery rule, and the deploy discipline.

    Failure modes to watch. A human logging into Skedda as the robot account still kills the fleet’s session until the next nightly run — the standing fix is a separate human admin user (Junyan, decided 2026-08-18; robot credentials live in Secret Manager only). alarm-mcp carries the same image-pin disease (alarm:arrivals-0818) and needs the same deploy-script treatment. And create_booking still 422s — now suspected anti-forgery/payload, not session, since edit_booking works; untested.

    Arrivals no longer depend on TELUS email — the panel’s own arm state is now the sourceCHANGE

    The board accused a renter of a no-show because a pipe was dead. Kiah Francis’ 07:30–09:30 in 509A rendered a red “⚠ no arrival” this morning. She had arrived. The board’s armEvents array was empty for the entire day, all five studios — the TELUS Secure Business feed on the Gmail label Artist Care - ADT went silent at 23:31 on 08-17 and stayed silent. Verified two ways (from:telus after:2026/08/18 and a sender-blind 509 newer_than:1d, so a labelling failure would still have shown): the mail does not exist. Not mislabelled, not in spam — absent.

    The failure mode is the point. A dead feed and a real no-show produced the identical output, arrived: null, and once a booking ends that renders as an accusation with a renter’s name on it. armFallback was false, so the board did not know anything was wrong. This is the second death of this feed.

    Alarm.com’s history API was the obvious fix and it does not work on this account. activity/historyEvents carries everything the emails carry, names included — but it is scoped to the session’s selected system, identity reports unitId 110267422 (Studio 901), and every read returns 901 only. Every documented switch endpoint 404s except systems/availableSystemItems/selectSystem, which 500s on all eight body shapes tried — the same server-side breakage this commercial account already shows on the group-level device catalog. filter[unitId], filter[locationIds] and OData filter=unitId eq N are all accepted and all silently ignored. Do not spend another session on this; it reproduces in minutes and the finding is recorded in alarm_monitor/arrivals.py.

    What shipped instead: the arm-state ledger. alarm-mcp’s watchdog tick already reads all five partitions every minute — the one cross-panel read this account does not break — and a transition is the fact the emails report, one hop upstream. armed → disarmed is an arrival; disarmed → armed is a departure. Each tick diffs against the previous and appends to a GCS-backed ledger (arm-history.json, its own object beside the watchdog’s, 72h retention so the cross-midnight tail survives). New read-only route GET /arm-history serves it. No new schedule, no new credential, no system switching.

    Two sources now, with different jobs. The panel owns the timeline (it stays up); the ADT email feed owns the actor names, which arm state cannot provide, and the sub-tick events state-polling misses. enrich_arm_names() matches a mail event to a panel event of the same studio and kind within two minutes and lends it the name. When the mail feed is dead you get times without names instead of nothing at all — back-to-back bookings in one studio then fall through to the matcher’s existing time-window pass, which has always handled ADT’s own nameless notices.

    And the guard, which matters as much as the fix. feed_is_down(): zero events across all five studios, five hours into the operating day, with no healthy panel feed — that is a broken pipe, not a quiet morning. The page then suppresses every no-arrival flag and says “arrival feed down” instead. Three conditions, each load-bearing: one event anywhere clears it; a healthy panel ledger legitimately returning nothing is believed (it holds no events before the day’s first disarm, and suppressing then would hide real no-shows); and the five-hour delay stops it crying wolf every morning before the studios open.

    Failure modes to watch. Resolution is the tick interval — a disarm and re-arm inside one minute is invisible to state polling, and only the email feed catches those. The ledger is best-effort by design: it rides the siren watchdog’s tick, and recording an arrival must never be why that tick fails to answer an alarm, so every error there is swallowed and logged. Its failure rule is therefore the opposite of ledger_store’s, which fails the tick rather than decide on empty state. And panel state has no actor, so an unmatched event stays attributed to nobody rather than to whoever was booked — a remote studio-account disarm must never become a renter’s arrival.

    ⚠ NOT YET DEPLOYED. Code committed and tested (16 new tests in alarm-monitor, 25 in fbs-monitor, all four existing suites green). Live until the alarm-mcp redeploy and the two GitHub secrets land: with ALARM_HISTORY_URL unset the builder behaves exactly as before — but the feed-down guard is armed either way, so the accusation half of the bug is fixed the moment fbs-monitor rebuilds.

    The ingestion path had no witness — fbs-watch now watches the ledger tooCHANGE

    Removing the duplicate crons this morning left cron-job.org as the only trigger, and nothing was watching it. If the ticker stalls, mail and texts keep arriving and simply stop being recorded — and an agent reading git-first canon sees no messages, which is indistinguishable from a renter who never wrote. That is the exact gap that ran ~24h on 2026-08-06.

    Correcting something stated earlier in this session: cron-job.org’s failure notification is NOT a workflow tripwire. It reports Successful (2.04 s) the instant GitHub returns 204 for the dispatch — that is the request being accepted, not the work happening. A workflow that fails ninety seconds later is invisible to it. This is the same lesson RUNTIMES.md already recorded from the Aug 6 incident (“every link reports success on the input”), restated here because it was briefly forgotten.

    So the ledger got a third watch inside fbs-watch, alongside the board and the messaging lane. It reads the Actions API for watchlist.yml and qa-review.yml and judges both age and conclusion — age catches a dead ticker, conclusion catches a run that fired and failed. Watching from Cloudflare means an Actions capacity incident (runs created, then failed) still reports honestly, because the API stays up while the runners do not. watchlist’s age test is gated to 08:30–22:59 Toronto since it sleeps overnight by design; qa-review is deliberately not gated — it runs 24/7, and that is what gives the ticker itself overnight coverage. Transitions only, so an outage is one Slack message rather than eighty.

    qa-review also learned to report its own failure. watchlist-refresh has filed deduped Run Error rows since Aug 6; qa-review never did, so a failed review surfaced only as a red tick and an email. Its checkout is conditional (idle ticks stay ~5s), so the new handler re-checks out for itself rather than assuming scripts/ is present.

    And the signal got un-buried. A Gmail filter now routes [junyanboon/fbs-monitor] CI mail out of the inbox to its own label — ~15 failures/week that made every other CI mail look like noise. Safe to hide precisely because that repo already has a witness (fbs-watch’s board check) and is public, so its failures cost nothing. desk-correspondence mail deliberately still lands in the inbox.

    Deliberately NOT built: a 🛑 Run Monitor row for these workflows. The Sentinel judges heartbeats written into 📊 Workflow Reports by RemoteTriggers; GitHub Actions workflows write no such row, so a monitor row would first need a heartbeat step added to each workflow — and would then be a second alarm for a path the Cloudflare watch already covers from outside GitHub’s failure domain, which the Sentinel (itself a RemoteTrigger) does not. Junyan’s standing “no duplicates” ruling applies. Revisit only if the watcher proves unreliable.

    Re-examined and closed 2026-08-18, with a risk knowingly accepted. The Notion-path alarm would have been the less reliable of the two: the RemoteTrigger fleet it rides was dead ~18–36h on 2026-08-16/17 (nine agents missing — Responder, Watchman, Doorman, Concierge, Analyst, Host, Timekeeper, Bookkeeper, Weatherman), while fbs-watch ran straight through the same window and posted a board alert + recovery on Aug 17. A backup that fails more often than the primary is not a backup.

    The real gap, named so it is not rediscovered as a surprise: nothing watches fbs-watch. It carries three watches now (board, messaging, ledger); if its Cloudflare cron stops or the Slack webhook is revoked, all three go quiet simultaneously — and quiet is exactly what healthy looks like. This is the Aug 6 feed-gap class one level up. It predates the ledger watch (it began the moment a second watch was added) but concentrating a third watch here made it worse. A mutual deadman was designed and NOT built: fbs-watch stamps each scheduled run into KV, GET / returns non-200 once that stamp passes ~45 min, and a cron-job.org job (failure notifications already on) polls it — cron-job.org watching Cloudflare while Cloudflare watches cron-job.org, two vendors, neither able to go silent unnoticed. Junyan’s call 2026-08-18: the single watcher is good enough for now, on the evidence that it already outlived a fleet-wide outage that took down nine agents. Build the deadman if fbs-watch is ever seen to miss a tick. Also still untested either way: the Slack webhook path itself, which stays unproven until something real fires.

GET / returns ledger.configured: true, faults: [], and live ages for both workflows (watchlist 1 min — mid-run, conclusion: null, correctly treated as neither pass nor fail; qa-review 16 min, success).     Failure mode to watch: the ledger watch needs a read-only GITHUB_TOKEN secret on the Worker. Unset, it skips silently and cleanly — which means an un-deployed or un-secreted watcher looks exactly like a healthy one from the outside. Confirm it is live by hitting the Worker’s GET /, which returns the current reading as JSON without posting to Slack: the ledger key must show configured: true and both workflows’ ages. If the PAT expires, the watch raises that as a fault rather than going quiet.

    A second unattended writer now exists for alarm codes and FBS rows — written, tested, switched OFFNEW

    The direct-booking one-shot lane (wayfinder ticket 081) is built. dc-canon/services/event-gate/booking_sweep.py, wired at POST /booking-sweep/<GATE_PATH_TOKEN> with ?dry_run=true for a read-and-report pass. Intended cadence: Cloud Scheduler every 30 min, 24/7. No LLM in the hot path — it reads the Skedda board directly (mail is pure echo; the corrupted read/unread bit is not consumed), then per booking sets up the Artist record, provisions verified alarm access through alarm-mcp's /provision, seeds the FBS row at T−2, and queues a confirmation at Pending Review.

    It keeps no processed-set. Every prerequisite is checked against the surface that owns it, so the pass is idempotent, loses nothing on a cold start, and does a step on the pass where it comes due. A processed-set has to be right forever; a derived check is right on every pass by construction.

    The single-writer seam, spelled out. send-engine/SameDay.gs owns bookings starting today or tomorrow — mail-triggered, acting within a minute, and holder of the only authorised unattended alarm-code send. The sweep therefore writes nothing inside that window. It still checks it, and raises a :sameday-gap Actions row when access is missing, so a SameDay outage reaches a human instead of being silently absorbed by a second writer. The Codex collision is the incident this rule exists to prevent.

    Failure modes it was taught to have. A failed Skedda read files a sweep-blind Run Error and never reads as an empty calendar. Every pass reports its result including zero. A fuzzy artist match is never merged; a panel/Artist-page code mismatch refuses rather than picking a side; two users under one name stop the lane and escalate naming which to keep and which to delete (/provision resolves people by name and cannot address one of two); a code that opens no panel is a failure, not a success; board rows are matched on studio+date+start so the Host's Calendar-keyed rows are found rather than duplicated. Open residue rows are never re-raised. Kill switch BOOKING_SWEEP_DISABLED, independent of GATE_DISABLED. Second amendment (Junyan, same day): positive selection. Anyone who makes an instant booking gets processed — selection stands on structural facts from the raw payload (Skedda type 1, non-recurring, holder present with email off the venueuser join), not a staff blocklist; the holder’s email now feeds the exact artist match. A stripped non-admin Skedda session (every holder nulled) fails loudly as sweep-blind instead of skipping the whole board while reporting a healthy pass.

    NOT deployed and NOT scheduled — deliberately. The lane writes to Alarm.com, and flipping on a production trigger is Junyan's call. Ship order is recorded in ticket 082: deploy with the kill switch held down, run one ?dry_run=true pass against the live board and read its acted list, then schedule and release. Until that happens nothing about the running desk has changed. A related fix is carried in ticket 083: /provision currently calls a name ambiguous when a codeless leftover shell matches it, which blocks the HTA alarm-code gate for a renter whose access is fine (Hanano I., Hannah Cho) — the ruling is ignore-not-reuse, in _verdict_of, so every caller gets it at once.

    Watchlist doubled to a 15-minute tick, and the Actions bill got a hard ceilingCHANGE

    Junyan’s call: the watchlist is the desk’s sense organ and 30 minutes of blindness is too long. The cron-job.org job watchlist-refresh moved from */30 8-22 * * * to */15 8-22 * * * (America/Toronto) — 30 runs/day to 60. The daytime window is deliberately unchanged; the desk still sleeps 23:00–07:59. Job retitled from “30-min tick” to “15-min tick” so the console stops lying about its own cadence.

    This deliberately exceeds the free tier, and that is the point. Post-duplicate-removal the repo runs ~2,100 min/month at 30-min; at 15-min it is ~3,700 against a 2,000-minute allowance. A $20/month account-level Actions budget now absorbs the ~$14 difference, with stop-usage-at-limit ticked — so a runaway loop halts Actions rather than billing unbounded. Note GitHub’s rule that usage before budget creation does not count in the current cycle, so the $20 started clean on 2026-08-18.

    cron-job.org is now a single point of failure, by construction. With the in-repo schedule: crons removed earlier today, nothing re-fires these workflows if the ticker stalls. Verified on the job: notify on failure after 1 failure and notify when disabled for too many failures are both ON. That mail is the tripwire — if it ever arrives, treat it as a desk outage, not a service blip.

    The sibling job was mislabelled by a factor of six — label corrected, cadence deliberately not. qa-review was titled “10-min tick” but runs 0 * * * *: hourly, on the hour, and around the clock with no daytime window, unlike the watchlist. Retitled qa-review hourly tick, 24/7 (quick-answer reviewer) so the console stops asserting something false. The schedule itself was NOT touched — whether hourly is a deliberate downgrade from 10 minutes or undocumented drift is still unknown, and that is a judgment about how fast quick-answer review needs to be, not a cleanup. Recorded here so the question survives the rename: anyone who believed that job turned around in 10 minutes has been wrong by up to an hour, possibly for a long time.

    Failure mode to watch: the watchlist now commits twice as often to desk-correspondence main, so the push-race against the message-ledger writers doubles in frequency. The commit step already retries with rebase six times and exits green on loss, so the expected symptom is a ::warning:: about a lost race rather than a red run — but a rising rate of those warnings means the race is getting tight and the retry budget should be revisited.

    Inbound WhatsApp and Instagram now reach a human — by email, within a minuteNEW

    Nothing pinged anyone when a customer messaged the studio. dance-annex-desk-webhook captures each WhatsApp/Instagram message into 📨 Inbound Messages and stops — no Slack, no push, nothing (grepped the worker: zero notification calls). The number has had no phone-app inbox since it moved to the Cloud API, so that Notion row is the only copy, and JOB 35 reads it on the Concierge's pass, up to two hours later. The cost, observed: 2026-08-16 14:21, Keti Sharif asked on WhatsApp for a working walkthrough link during her booking. Nobody saw it. At 15:06 she wrote “Found it!” — she had solved it herself after 45 minutes.

    New route: send-engine/InboundNotify.gs, Apps Script, own time-driven trigger (~1 min), emailing the studio inbox per message with the text, sender, received time, a link to the row, and the 24-hour free-form reply deadline — the one thing in that message that expires. Junyan's ruling 2026-08-18: email, not Slack, because that is where he already triages.

    Why Apps Script and not the worker. The worker has no email path; adding one meant Cloudflare Email Routing (a dashboard verification, and the wrangler token here has no zone scope to check whether the zone even supports it) or a new third-party mail API. The Apps Script send engine already emails the studio inbox for its own alerts — a proven path, zero new credentials. It is a separate file on its own trigger, sharing only script properties: this project deploys by clipboard paste, and re-pasting the file that sends every customer message to add a notifier is not a trade worth making.

    Design choices that will matter at 2am. Email is sent before the row is ticked Notified, so a crash duplicates a notification rather than losing it. A self-installing watermark (INBOUND_NOTIFY_SINCE) means switching it on emails nothing on the first run instead of replaying 23 rows of history. A 24-hour age floor stops an outage dumping yesterday's traffic on recovery, and 10-per-pass keeps a burst from timing out mid-loop. New Notified checkbox added to 📨 Inbound Messages.

    Also corrected in canon: JOB 35 listed “No monitor” as a known gap — untrue since 2026-08-12, when fbs-watch began reading the worker's /status every 15 min. Its latency note now warns the Concierge that a message may already have been answered by hand, which makes the pre-draft Correspondence Log check load-bearing (dc-canon dbb3863b).

    LIVE and proven unattended 2026-08-18 14:20Z. A row planted at 14:16:21Z emailed itself at 14:19:59Z — 3m38s, one cycle, editor untouched.

    Getting there took two false positives, both the same shape. The Apps Script Triggers modal accepted “Save” and created nothing — the page still read “Showing 1 trigger” (main) hours later, so the notifier only ran when a human pressed Run. Hence installInboundTrigger(): it creates the trigger in code and logs the project's trigger list read back afterwards, because a save that appears to work may not have. Then the first “unattended” test looked like a pass — row emailed and ticked — until the clock was checked: it fired 23 minutes before the trigger existed, because someone had pressed Run. An unattended test planted before the install timestamp proves nothing, and the same lesson has now cost this fleet three separate jobs (the Doorman's single-fire cron, the Send-Timing Verifier's hour-early local cron, this).

    Every desk-correspondence tick was billed twice — the GitHub schedule: crons are goneFIX

    GitHub warned that the account had burned 90% of its 2,000 monthly Actions minutes with 16 days still to run. The cause was duplication, not growth. desk-correspondence is the only private repo in the fleet, so it is the only one that spends the allowance at all — day-hub, sdm-logger, fbs-monitor and day-sheet are public and run free. Every minute of the overrun came from one repo.

    Both pollers were firing twice per tick. watchlist-refresh and qa-review each carried a schedule: cron and were being fired by cron-job.org over workflow_dispatch. Measured across Aug 6–18: watchlist 362 dispatch vs 203 schedule, qa-review 281 vs 142. About 38% of all runs were exact duplicates doing work that had just been done minutes earlier.

    The external ticker is the reliable half, so it kept the job. cron-job.org delivered watchlist 30/day and qa-review 24/day, every single day in the window, with no gap. CLAUDE.md already records that GitHub schedule: is unreliable fleet-wide and fit only for jobs tolerating multi-hour gaps — so the crons were paying full price for a backstop that was never trustworthy. workflow_dispatch is now the only trigger on both workflows; cron-job.org is the tick of record.

    The red runs were a guardrail working, not a bug. 36 failed watchlist runs burned real minutes, but every one traced to the canon completeness check refusing a new library/*.md that had no MIRRORS row — first the-loop.md (Aug 7), then job-35-messaging-triage.md (Aug 12). Each was corrected by hand within hours and nothing has failed since Aug 12. The check is doing exactly its job; the cost is that it fails late in the run, after ~10 Python steps have already been paid for.

    Failure mode to watch: with schedule: removed there is no in-repo re-fire if cron-job.org stalls. A silent ticker is now a silent feed gap — which is precisely the shape that let the 2026-08-06 outage run ~24h. The run-error rows and the monitor path are the only things standing between a stopped ticker and a quiet-looking day, so treat a sudden absence of watchlist commits as an outage until proven otherwise.

    Board takes Skedda only — the marketplace calendar lane is now ignored at ingestCHANGE

    Junyan’s call, an hour after the lane diagnosis: stop consuming the second lane at all. Peerspace and Giggster each push their own event into the studio calendars alongside the Skedda→Google mirror. The Skedda copy carries room, price, paid status and tracks moves; the marketplace copy carries a confirmation number and structurally cannot move. Taking both is what produced a renter in two studios (Jessica T., 509B + 693) and a renter named “Booking on Giggster.com https”.

    This replaces yesterday’s tiebreak with a policy. The 01:05Z entry resolved marketplace-vs-Skedda conflicts; drop_marketplace_mirrors() now ignores marketplace events outright at ingest, before anything merges. _mark_cross_studio_dupes is back to marking only — with mirrors gone, a surviving two-studio pair is something the builder genuinely cannot adjudicate (two Skedda rows = a real double-booking; a hand-made event that means something to staff), so it still keeps both rows and the amber badge.

    Twinning needs two rules, and both earn their place. By renter name, because a moved booking’s mirror sits in the wrong room — “Peerspace Booking, Jessica T.” on 509B matched to “Jessica T. Peerspace” on 693. By room, because a marketplace title often carries no person at all (“Giggster Booking” reduces to zero name tokens) and a studio holds one booking at a time. Name-only would have left every Giggster mirror on the board; room-only would have left every moved Peerspace mirror.

    The guard that matters more than the feature. A marketplace booking with no Skedda counterpart is kept and reported as ⚠ … NO Skedda booking. That is a hole in the system of record, and an empty studio on the board is exactly how a sync gap turns into somebody standing at a locked door. Verified against today’s live calendars: three mirrors ignored (Jessica 509B→693 moved, ALVIN 509B same-room, Welton 693 same-room), zero orphans, every surviving row Skedda-sourced.

    Failure mode to watch: the ⚠ NO Skedda booking line. One means a marketplace booking never reached Skedda — act on it, do not treat it as board noise. Separately, if Skedda ever stops prefixing descriptions with Spaces:, every row falls back to unknown, no mirror is recognised, and the duplicates return — noisy and visible, never silent.

    The board reads Google calendars, not Skedda — and each one has TWO writers. Yesterday’s entries blamed a stale ICS cache. Wrong, and the calendar API settles it. Studio 509B holds Peerspace Booking, Jessica T., created 2026-07-24, never updated, description carrying a Peerspace confirmation number and manage-link. Studio 693 holds Jessica T. Peerspace (Studio 693) [Paid], created 2026-08-15 11:58Z, description Spaces: Studio 693. Both are real, current calendar entries. One is the Skedda→Google mirror; the other is Peerspace’s own feed, which lands on whichever studio the listing names and never learns about a move, because moves happen in Skedda.

    So the pair was never ambiguous — the builder just wasn’t reading the evidence. lane_of() now classifies each event from its DESCRIPTION (read for provenance only, never rendered — descriptions carry prices): skedda when it starts Spaces: Studio NNN, marketplace on a Peerspace/Giggster/Tagvenue/Splacer link, else unknown. When a cross-studio pair holds exactly one Skedda row, the marketplace row is dropped and the drop is logged as a NOTE: line. That is not picking a winner between two equal claims: it is one source that tracks moves and one that structurally cannot.

    Everything else still asks the reader. Two Skedda rows disagreeing is a genuine double-booking, not a ghost. An unknown-lane row may be a hand-made event that means something to staff. Both shapes keep both rows, keep the amber “moved? verify”, and keep the flag counted. Only marketplace rows are ever removed.

Giggster simply won on length. The cache-buster is still correct and still worth having — it just did not do this.

Every marketplace booking also exists in Skedda (Jessica 117983958, ALVIN 118923428, Welton 118386232), with the room, the price and the paid status. The marketplace event adds only a confirmation number and a manage-link — and it is the sole cause of both defects chased this week. Switching that sync off would remove the class outright; the board would lose nothing. Not done — that is production sync config and Junyan’s call.

Every drop prints NOTE: dropped stale marketplace row <who> on <studio> — Skedda has it in <studio> in the run log; if a renter is missing and no such line exists, the cause is elsewhere. And if Skedda ever starts writing descriptions without the Spaces: prefix, every row falls back to unknown and the board reverts to asking — noisy, never wrong.

    Reports tab grows a Headline lane — and the Custodian stops crying wolfCHANGE

    The Reports tab listed which jobs ran but not what they said. Its stated purpose is the few-words answer — “is something wrong that needs me?” — but most fleet reports title themselves The Sentinel — 2026-08-15, so the tab read as a attendance sheet. The body can never be pulled onto the board (public page, rule 5), so the answer needed a dedicated public-safe field.

    New field, new contract. 📊 Workflow Reports gains a Headline rich-text property. Canon (dc-canon library/autonomy.md §Run Standard item 1) now requires every run, every pass, to fill it: one line, ≤120 chars, “what happened · what needs a human”, prefixed ⚠ if and only if something needs a human now — a decorative ⚠ trains the reader to ignore the real ones. Rolling agents overwrite it each pass, keeping a standing ⚠ until the underlying item is resolved, not merely reported. Explicitly public: counts, studios, first names fine; never codes, PINs, amounts, contact details.

    Board renders it on both editions. The headline gets a full-width wrapping line under the run title; a ⚠ headline shows amber-bold and lifts the row to watch even on a Completed run — status and findings are different axes, and a job can finish cleanly having found something wrong. The tab's summary bar now says “N runs flagged something” when no run died but some run raised a hand. Rows without the field render exactly as before, so the tab degrades to its old shape until the fleet starts filling it — expect headlines to appear over the next day's passes as each agent re-reads canon.

    Same session, the Custodian's chronic false red. Its 🚥 Run Monitor row carried Cadence 2×/day with Stale after 60 min — a dead-man threshold sized for an hourly robot on a schedule that fires 09:35 + 02:35 with a 17-hour gap, so the row went red every afternoon by construction. The run itself was fine (checked in 10:15). Stale after is now 1140 min (19 h), sized to the real gap like the Daily rows: a missed 09:35 goes red ~21:35 same evening, a missed 02:35 by the 08:00 window-open. The reasoning is written into the row's Expected field so nobody tunes it back down.

    Failure mode to watch: headlines that go stale on rolling reports (yesterday's ⚠ carried after the fix landed) — the canon rule says newest-wins per pass, so a headline older than the report's Completed At by a full pass means that agent is not re-reading §Run Standard.

    Alarm-trigger detection moves to the event gate's minute tick — the Concierge exits the alarm businessCHANGE

    A real alarm used to wait up to 2 hours for an LLM pass to read two emails. New alarm_watch.py in event-gate rides the existing event-gate-poll minute tick: it reads alarm-mcp's live /panel-states and treats a partition's in_alarm false→true edge as the trigger — detection in ~1 minute, no email parsing at all.

    On an edge it does three things, none judgment: pages on-call by SMS (same Phone.com summons as the emergency lane), files a Staff Call Queue row, and raises the Alarm trigger Studio <NNN> Customer Care ticket at Position = Gate: Staff, deduped per studio+day.     Deployed the same night — and the deploy took the whole gate down for ~4.5 hours. The env command written for Junyan used --set-env-vars, which replaces the entire environment rather than adding to it, so setting the two alarm variables deleted the other twelve — GATE_PATH_TOKEN among them. Every route authenticates by path token, so the gate 404'd on every scheduler tick from ~21:45 to ~02:15 Toronto: no emergency SMS forwarding, no quick-answer cards, no invariants, no loop. Restored from an env dump taken before the deploy. Every inbound message in the window was audited afterwards: two from Aakanksha (Junyan was replying live), two from Kyle about moving a Sept 6 booking (ordinary triage, captured by the ledger), one thanks, one Giggster relay. Nothing emergency-shaped arrived — luck, not design. Use --update-env-vars/--update-secrets; dump the env before touching it. Logged in RUNTIMES.

    Then the watch itself failed 267 reads in a row, silently, for two reasons. (1) The mcp-auth-token secret version carries a trailing newline, and Python's http client rejects a header value containing one — the request never left the process, so a perfectly valid credential looked like an outage. Fixed by stripping on read, in alarm_watch.py and in presence.py, which carried the identical latent bug and only became reachable now that these two variables exist at all. (2) The lane's own “I am blind” row could never file: Alarm Watch was not an option on ✅ Actions to Perform's Raised by select, so the write 400'd. The lane failed and its failure report failed — the exact silent-failure shape this design exists to prevent. Option added; both fixes committed (dc-canon 3d5c1a51, 318 tests green) and awaiting one more deploy.

    First verified send happened this session. Two attempts taught the lane's rules the hard way: a body-only row takes the worker's free-form path, which requireOpenWindow refuses outside the 24-hour customer-service window (Keti had never messaged the line), so the row flipped Error. Re-released with Template = hta_studio_access it dispatched clean: Sent stamped 22:20Z, walkthrough video looked up live. An email copy went to her address as well.

    Correction to this entry's first version, which said that failure was silent. It was not. markError writes the reason into the row's Reasoning property (not a comment, which is where the SMS engine puts it — that difference is what fooled the reader here), and recordFailure writes a fail:send:* key that fbs-watch turns into a Slack alert on the next tick. The reason was overwritten by the retry before anyone read it, and its absence was then mistaken for absence of reporting. Read Reasoning before re-releasing an errored WhatsApp row — it is the only copy.

    What changed where: the FBS skill page's PENDING block now reads LIVE with the routing rule (non-+1 number → never SMS; WhatsApp template + email copy), and a channel check was added above the HTA step. Canon's house-rules.md 🌍 section was already current from the 08-11/08-12 sessions; response-resolver.md still said the lane was “reserved, not yet live” and was corrected (dc-canon d1245cf4).

    Propagated to every send surface (same day, second pass): canon the-responder.md, job-1-adt-alarm.md, process-studio-tickets-full.md (dc-canon 097f0cab) — PBF/HTA to a non-+1 number now reads WhatsApp-template-first, Email fallback; Notion Skill: Send Message bumped to v8 with full WhatsApp/Instagram channel branches (who dispatches what, template vs free-form, Reasoning as the error surface); the 🗺️ SOP Mapping 🌍 callout's standing rule flipped from “redirect to Email” to “WhatsApp, then Email” and its stale International-reserved and WhatsApp-not-polled bullets replaced; SOPs Changing Records / Email / Number and Need Artist's Contact Information re-routed their country-code checks. A two-query sweep of the SOPs wiki found no other page carrying its own copy of the rule.

And a Zapier-side WhatsApp Business app was enabled during diagnosis before the real pipe was found — to be disabled so the map keeps one WhatsApp path.

  • 2026-08-16 · 00:10Z

    The FBS Monitor now reads Skedda — renter names only, and only the ones the feeds omitNEW

    A marketplace booking arrives with no person on it. Giggster’s ICS summary is Booking on Giggster.com https://giggster.com/… — the platform, not the renter. Two defects rode on that. First, clean_who splits Name: Description on the first colon and the https: colon got there first, so the board rendered a renter called “Booking on Giggster.com https” (Welton R. Giggster’s 693 booking, 118386232, 19:30–22:30). Fixed by stripping URLs before the split and collapsing the boilerplate to Giggster Booking. Second, and unfixable from the feed: the renter’s name is not in the ICS at all. Skedda has it.

    New route: fbs-monitor → Skedda, read-only. skedda_names.py scrapes the anti-forgery token and issues two GETs (/webs, /bookingslists). Matched on studio + overlapping window, a nameless platform title is replaced with Skedda’s renter (user.name, else the booking title — where a casual marketplace booking carries the person). Only nameless titles are touched — “Peerspace Booking, Jessica T.” and “ALVIN W. Peerspace” already name someone and are left alone — and only on exactly one match: two candidates means the read cannot tell them apart, and a confident wrong name at the door does more damage than an honest “Giggster Booking”.

    Why a partial copy instead of importing skedda-cli. Canon is skedda-cli (skedda/client.py, 1169 lines); this copies ~80 of them — the token scrape and two reads. The rest of that file creates, edits, cancels and re-holders bookings. This builder runs unattended every 15 minutes on a schedule nobody watches; it should be structurally unable to write to Skedda, not merely disinclined. Drift is the accepted cost and the file says so at the top.

    Auth rides existing plumbing, so nothing expires on a two-week clock. The cookie comes from Secret Manager (skedda-cookie, project danceannex-skedda), refilled daily 06:00 Toronto by the skedda-refresh Cloud Run job. The workflow authenticates with a new GCP_SA_KEY repo secret and passes the cookie as SKEDDA_COOKIE. Without that secret both steps skip and the board builds exactly as before — verified against a bad cookie, a missing gcloud, and a board with no nameless titles (which never calls Skedda at all). A nicety, never a dependency.

    Deliberately NOT doing the tempting second thing. Skedda also knows which studio a booking is in, which would settle yesterday’s cross-studio ghost properly. It cannot be used for that yet: this read drops recurring series (Skedda stamps a series with its first occurrence, so today’s occurrence is absent from the response), and an absent booking is indistinguishable from a moved one. Wiring studio authority onto a read that silently omits every weekly renter would turn contract bookings into ghosts. That needs the recurrence expansion skedda-cli already has.

    Also fixed: the tests were never running. _selftest_dedupe had no caller anywhere — including for yesterday’s duplicate-marking work. Now test_titles.py, wired into CI beside the ADT guards: 8 title shapes, the nameless-title rule, and the dedupe cases.

    Failure mode to watch: a board reading “Giggster Booking” again means the lookup is skipping — the run log carries Skedda name lookup skipped with the reason, usually an expired cookie the daily job failed to refresh, or the GCP key. The board stays correct either way; it just stops being helpful.

  • 2026-08-15 · 16:15Z

    Job D can now fix what it finds — and the board grew a Reports tab to say soCHANGE

    The daily code check could detect drift but never repair it, by construction. /code-match answers match/differs/unknown and deliberately never returns a digit, which is what lets the unattended cloud Doorman run it without a PIN entering an agent transcript. The cost of that guarantee: a differs was unfixable by the thing that found it, so every correction waited on an attended local session. New route POST /code-mirror on alarm-mcp closes it from the other side — that service already holds the Alarm.com credential, so it reads the code, writes it into the Artist DB, and drops it, returning counts and names only. Both routes judge through one shared code_sweep.sweep(), so the verdict-only and correcting surfaces cannot drift apart; a single strip_codes() call is what separates them.

    It acts on one state and counts the rest. Both sides hold a code and they differ — that is the state that texts somebody a PIN which will not open the door. “No panel user” is normal and is never a finding (owner ruling 2026-08-14): the Artist DB keeps a code after the panel user is retired so a returning renter gets the number they already remember, and 534 of 696 rows are in exactly that state. An earlier build reported all 534 as findings, which is a 534-line report nobody reads. Two states that no machine can resolve — one name matching two panel users, and panels disagreeing with each other — go to a human, and nothing ever blanks a populated field, because an empty Alarm Code sends an HTA with a hole in it.

    A real bug fell out of the port. judge_claims called find_codes once per claim, and find_codes walks the entire paginated Alarm.com roster — so the 696-name sweep /code-match was built for meant 696 roster walks, several thousand requests. It was never noticed because the route had only ever been called with a handful of claims. The roster is now fetched once and deviceAccess memoised per user id; matching semantics are unchanged.

    Scheduling — and who owns it. A dedicated alarm-code-mirror Cloud Scheduler job was built first, then dropped on Junyan’s ruling: no duplicates, all under the Doorman. Job D is now a single code_mirror(write=true) call in the Doorman’s existing ~06:00 pass, so there is one owner and one schedule rather than a cron running beside an agent doing the same check. The Doorman owns the job without ever holding a digit — the tool reads the code inside the alarm service and hands back counts and names. Its old B(b4) block, which could only detect and file a row for a human, is rewritten; the load-bearing rule there is never raise a row about something in fixed, since that is precisely the duplicate this removes. The plain-HTTP route survives for non-MCP callers and manual runs.

    Downstream: the run files its one-liner as a 📊 Workflow Reports row, and the FBS Monitor gained a Reports tab that renders those rows — Robots answers did it run?, Reports answers what did it say?, and a job can check in perfectly on time having found something wrong. The tab reads titles only, never bodies: titles are written to be published, bodies carry renter names, and that board is a public GitHub Pages site. The board also gained a service worker so it stays readable when the connection drops — network-first for pages, and version.json is never cached, so an offline copy can never report itself as current.

    Proven, and the test earned its keep on the first run. deploy/_mirror_selftest.py creates a throwaway Artist row pointed at a staff panel account, gives it a deliberately wrong code, and watches the mirror correct it — panels never written, no renter record touched, row archived afterwards. First run: checked 698 · matched 163 · corrected 1, audit line present. It also immediately caught a real bug — a missing leading slash in the audit-trail write, which sits inside the correction loop and is uncaught, so the first genuine correction would have written the code and then aborted the whole sweep on a 400. Three clean dry runs could never have found it: that line only executes when something actually changes.

  • 2026-08-15 · 16:05Z

    A moved booking can show in two studios at once — the ghost no longer claims a no-showFIX

    Studio identity on the FBS Monitor comes only from which ICS feed an event arrived in. build.py tags each event with the key of the ICS_URL_<studio> feed it was fetched from. So a booking moved between studios does not move on the board until the old space’s feed drops it — a stale feed is a wrong studio, not merely a late one. Seen today: Jessica T. Peerspace, 09:00–11:00, rendered on both 509B and 693. Skedda held exactly one booking (117983958, Studio 693); the 509B row was a ghost.

    The double row was not the damage. The ghost had no arrival and its window had passed, so it drew the red “⚠ no arrival” flag and counted toward the header’s flag total — the board asserting a no-show for a booking that had been running normally one room over.

    Two changes, both in fbs-monitor. _bust_cache appends a timestamp parameter and sends Cache-Control: no-cache on every ICS fetch, so an HTTP-layer stale copy stops being a source of this. _mark_cross_studio_dupes then runs after the existing same-studio dedupe: overlapping booking events for the same renter in different studios each record the other’s studio in dup_studios. Both templates render “⚠ also in <studio> — moved? verify” in amber in place of the red no-arrival line.

    Marked, never dropped. The builder cannot tell which of the two rows Skedda now considers real — the feeds are its only source of studio identity — so deleting one would be a guess written as fact. The row stays on the board and stays counted in the flag total; only the colour and the claim change. Person matching is a token-set overlap requiring two shared name tokens (_same_renter), because the positional _renter_key cannot link “Peerspace Booking, Jessica T.” to “Jessica T. Peerspace”, and a one-token rule would let a different Jessica’s genuine no-show be recast as a move. Four cases added to _selftest_dedupe; both views verified against a fixture payload.

    Failure mode to watch: if the cache-buster fixes the staleness at source, this badge should become rare — a board showing it routinely means the old space’s feed is stale on Skedda’s side, not in transit, and the durable fix is reading space from the Skedda API instead of feed provenance.

    An access grant that reads back “4 panels” can still be Existing-only — the count is now not enoughFIX

    A panel count proves EXISTING access only. grant_access verified itself by re-reading systemsWithAccessCount and treating 4 as a full grant. An Existing-only user opens today’s four panels and silently misses any access point added later — a lockout dated to whenever the next panel is installed, which is nobody’s watch. hasExistingPlusFutureAccess was already in the same deviceAccesses/{uid} payload the count came from, and nothing read it. Found on Roux Buciu’s rebuild today: the grant returned 4 panels, Access Type came back Existing-only, and Junyan corrected it by hand in the UI.

    Both layers now require the flag. alarm-monitor 3c9793b: preflight_verdict returns manual_no_access when all_existing_plus_future is False (placed after the zero-panel check and before the window logic, so it gates permanent users too, not only windowed ones), and grant_access raises instead of reporting success on an Existing-only read-back. None stays “not reported”, never False — same convention as the 2026-08-08 zero-panel check. Regression test added, 91 pass. dc-canon 7ee62520: gates.md now requires a non-zero panel count and the flag for any access gate. The repo had no remote and now does — junyanboon/danceannex-alarm-monitor, private, all 22 commits pushed. History was audited before the first push: .env was never tracked (it has been in .gitignore since the start), and no code, panel credential or device token appears in any blob — every match is a variable name, a .env.example placeholder, or a vendored upstream test fixture. It stays private because this repo writes door access.

    The window half, corrected. Yesterday’s ruling — “At All Times” is the source of truth for coverage — held for a vestigial window and was written into canon on that basis. The raw payload then showed Roux’s temporarySchedule was genuinely stored (2026-07-15T19:10Z → 2026-08-15T19:10Z, credentialStatus 3 — settled, not mid-write) while Salia Magno and Aaron AquinoAnnobil both read null. So the UI edit never persisted to the account and the expired-user sweep would have deleted her at Sat Aug 15 15:10 EDT, the mechanism that removed five artists on 2026-08-08. A 🛑 EXCEPTION was added to gates.md (b9769db2): a populated schedule with credentialStatus 3 overrides the ruling and must be raised. The tool was never misreading anything — an earlier defect row claiming it read Access Period wrongly was withdrawn and rewritten.

    What actually cleared it. Deleting the access code did not clear the window — a re-read still returned the same schedule on user 245811149, now with systems_with_access: 0. Only deleting the user removed it. Recreated as 247758969 with window_days = 0; check_access_covers for her Aug 30 Studio 901 booking now returns allow_permanent, both window fields null. Never write a window near the access grant on this account — the panels sync after the grant call returns and discard it, which is the likeliest origin of the bad record. create_user already defaults to no window and warns about exactly this.

    Failure mode to watch: the MCP server runs the old code until it is restarted, so check_access_covers keeps passing Existing-only users until then. Separately, whether the expiry sweep keys off the stored record or the effective policy is still unresolved — the exposure was removed rather than observed. The operating check is unchanged and now proven twice: temporarySchedule: null, not a screenshot reading “At All Times”.

    The walkthrough link had two sources; now it has one, and the WhatsApp sender is under version controlFIX

    The hardcoded map is gone. STUDIO_VIDEO in dance-annex-desk-webhook/src/send.js listed a YouTube id per studio while the SMS path read the same link live from 🎦 YouTube Videos. Two sources that never read each other — so updating the database left WhatsApp sending a retired video until someone remembered to deploy, and nothing anywhere reported the divergence. The WhatsApp path now reads the same database on every send. To change a walkthrough, edit the database. There is no longer an ID in the code.

    Read fresh per send, deliberately uncached. A walkthrough is re-recorded perhaps twice a year, so the extra Notion call costs nothing worth counting, and a cache would reintroduce exactly the staleness the change removes.

    It fails the row instead of guessing. No live walkthrough for the studio, more than one, an empty URL, or a URL with no readable id — each throws, the row goes to Error with the reason, and the failure is visible in Notion. That is a real behaviour change: the old map always had an answer, including a wrong one. A silently wrong walkthrough is a renter at a locked door, so a failed row is the better outcome. The lookup takes the one row for that studio ending — HTA and not marked (OBSOLETE; equipment clips are tagged to the same rooms, so the suffix is what identifies a renter-facing video. Verified before deploy: exactly one live row per studio across all five.

    The repo was not a repo. The worker was being deployed from an untracked directory — the only copies of its source were one Mac and Cloudflare. Now git init’d with a .gitignore and an initial commit (757fd02), plus npm test covering the lookup against a fixture of the real row mix. No remote yet — pushing it somewhere is Junyan’s call. Deployed version 780b2e75-6ef0-4b2a-8a8f-53a2e60c9bd8.

    Still open: a multi-studio HTA carries two walkthroughs and a WhatsApp URL button holds one, and Studio on the queue row is a single select — so combined bookings still have no rule. Unchanged by this work.

    Provisioning Kosi Ese produced a ten-minute window where every read disagreed. create_user returned 200, the API roster listed her with code 3943 in four panel slots, and the Alarm.com Users search returned “No Users”.     Rule: absence from the UI in the first few minutes is not evidence the create failed. Wait ~10 minutes before checking, and never re-create a user because the list looks empty. That is the most plausible origin of the duplicate Kristyan Calletor pair now in the account — a code-less shell (247757468) beside the complete record (247689442). Same eventual-consistency behaviour as the panel’s known ~9–13 minute delay on bypass changes, on a different surface.

    The real defect the wait exposed: Kosi’s Access Type reads All Existing Devices. Aaron Aquino-Annobil and Kristyan Calletor both read Existing + Future. create_user documents itself as granting “every access point on every panel (Existing + Future)”, and check_access_covers returns all_existing_plus_future: true for her — so the API asserts a grant level the panel does not show.

    Nothing is broken today: four panels exist and she is on all four. A panel or access point added later would silently exclude her — the same quiet-drift class that left four artists carrying PINs that opened nothing. Fixed by hand for Kosi. Worth auditing every user this tool has created, and worth fixing grant_user_access so the grant matches its own documentation.

    Both filed on Sweep row 3bd7503281c481ffa584e13e99dafb77, alongside the access_type/code_available defect from earlier the same day.

    The Weatherman can finally raise its hand — and now it does it where someone is lookingNEW SIGNAL

    Step 8d has been dead since it was written. The Weatherman’s trigger carried Notion, Google Calendar, Google Drive, Gmail and IFTTT — and no Slack connector at all.     Then the harder question, which is Junyan’s: where should an issue go so it actually gets picked up? Sweep Feedback is the durable record and stays the destination — but it is a backlog, not an inbox. 188 rows sat at New on 2026-08-14. Anything filed there alone is preserved, not noticed, and a thermostat drifting mid-booking is an hour-scale problem.

    Alerts now go to #desk-status (C0BNPTLMZV2) — the machine-health channel fbs-watch already alerts into, whose entire purpose is “a piece of the plumbing is broken”. It is the one channel someone reads with that question already in mind.

    Why not the two channels canon named before. #desk-drops is an input queue — staff type observations in, JOB 4’s drop scan reads them out. Posting machine alerts there mixes the desk’s input with its output, and JOB 4 is routinely skipped or part-run for budget, so the alert can simply sit. #all-dance-annex-studios is the staff-wide room, where a thermostat notice is noise to everyone and the queue of nobody.

    A ping is not a task. When a finding needs a human hand — a filter changed, a unit looked at, a landlord called — the Slack line is the alert and an ✅ Actions to Perform row (For = Ops Lead, Type = Studio Issue) is the assignment. That row is now the sole exception to 8b’s “Sweep Feedback only” rule alongside 9a, and it exists because a channel message nobody owns is how a broken unit stays broken.

    Canon library/climate-controller.md §8b/§8d (8327cca9), including a note to check mcp_connections before rereading the wording if the step ever fails again. Two Sweep rows closed.

    The prompt fix nobody could apply was never a UI job — Responder v1.2 written over the APIFIX

    Both Responder triggers carried a line that contradicted canon: “Junyan’s test line (647-773-3887) is always a TEST — never draft to it.” Canon says the opposite, twice — the-responder.md §78 (“the earlier ‘never draft to it’ reading was wrong and cost a pass”) and response-resolver.md §63 (“drafting is correct”) — both from Junyan’s 2026-07-30 ruling settling a live disagreement between this lane and the event-gate lane, which drafted while the Responder refused.

    It was flagged for 11 consecutive days. Sweep Feedback rows on Aug 4, 7, 8 and 11, and a standing Fixer Ledger item reading “prompt paste — canon can’t reach the UI”. Every one of those entries filed it as work only Junyan could do by hand.

    That premise was wrong. RemoteTrigger update writes the prompt over the API. The documented limitation — that granting an environment access to desk-correspondence is a manual click with no API — is real, but it was over-generalised into “the whole prompt is UI-only”. Anything in job_config.ccr.events is agent-writable. Both triggers now run prompt v1.2, carrying the canon rule plus its two hard limits: drafts to the test line stay held at Pending Review, and the line never becomes a real renter downstream (no ticket, no billing, no CRM, no Artist DB).

    ⚠ Mechanics worth keeping. update is a partial update, but job_config replaces wholesale — get first and resend the complete ccr block (environment_id + events + session_context) with only the intended edit changed. mcp_connections, cron_expression and enabled are siblings and survive untouched; both triggers were read back to confirm their five connectors and cron were intact.

    The general lesson is the one to keep. An item parked as “human-only” is a claim about a tool surface, and tool surfaces change. This one sat for eleven days behind an assumption nobody re-tested. Registry updated at RUNTIMES.md with the API path and the partial-update gotcha, so the next reader does not re-derive it.

    The access checker says “no access, no code” about users holding a live PIN on four panelsDEFECT

    Aaron Aquino-Annobil had a paid 19:00 Studio 901 booking. check_access_covers returned access_type: none, systems_with_access: 0, code_available: false, verdict manual_no_access, with the instruction to grant access before sending any access message. find_user_codes agreed: panels_covered: 0, code_slots: {}. On that read I told Junyan he would be standing at a locked door in three and a half hours.

    The panel says otherwise. The Alarm.com page for the same user shows Access Code 4907, Access Period At All Times, Access Type Existing + Future, Locations All. He was fully provisioned the whole time and always had been.

    Why this shape is dangerous. The code value read correctly — 4907 matched the panel exactly. Only the coverage fields were false. So the connector looks healthy, returns a confident verdict, and the one field the verdict turns on is silently wrong. A false manual_no_access sends the Doorman down the hold-and-gate path for a renter who can already get in: it manufactures an access emergency out of nothing, and it trains the desk to discount a correct verdict later.

    Interim rule until this is diagnosed: manual_no_access is not sufficient grounds to hold an access message. Confirm against the Alarm.com UI first. Worth checking whether the coverage fields come from a call that fails soft and returns 0 rather than raising, and whether the fault is per-user or account-wide — compare panels_covered against the UI for two or three known-good users.

    Narrowed the same day, on a second user — the broad claim above is withdrawn. Provisioning Kosi Ese produced access_type: none and code_available: false alongside a correct systems_with_access: 4, with get_user_code showing code 3943 live in four panel slots. So the coverage count is not broadly unreliable; Aaron’s 0 may have been true at that moment and fixed before the screenshot. What reproduces across both users is exactly two fields: access_type and code_available, which contradict systems_with_access inside the same response.

    Practical rule: do not gate an access message on access_type or code_available. Use systems_with_access / panels_covered, the verdict, and get_user_code’s slot table. The verdict logic itself survives the bug — allow_permanent is derived from the absence of a window, not from these fields — but anything reading them directly reads the opposite of the truth.

    Logged as an attributed defect against J07 Doorman (Sweep row 3bd7503281c481ffa584e13e99dafb77). No message was withheld and no wrong code was sent in either case.

    Staff can now tell the desk a renter forgot to arm — one line in #desk-dropsNEW SIGNAL

    The gap: an arm event at 8:03 PM looks identical whether the renter set it or staff armed remotely 25 minutes later because the renter forgot. The Overtime skill already handles this honestly — “unnamed arm = staff remote login, never flag”, then route the ambiguity to the Ops Lead as a Review. It behaves correctly and still produces a steady drip of human decisions, because it has no signal that resolves them. On 2026-08-14 three of four open overtime Reviews were this exact shape (Gaby Bondoc 509B Aug 7, Gabrielle Curate 527 Aug 11, Kristel San Jose 901 Aug 3) and all three were settled by Junyan phoning the renter.     The signal. Staff on FBS support type one line in Slack #desk-drops at close-up: noarm <studio> <renter> — e.g. noarm 527 Gabby. Phone-native, no app, no login, no form; staff surface was the deciding constraint.

    Inbox vs record. Slack is the inbox; the booking is the record. New Closed by select on the 🛎️ FBS AI Support board — Renter · Staff — renter forgot to arm · Staff — scheduled close · Unknown — no signal — sits beside the existing Armed/Disarmed fields. Nobody should re-read a chat log months later to learn why a booking was not charged. It also makes the repeat-offender pattern countable for the first time: the Forgot-to-Arm SOP has always said to escalate “repeated forgot-to-arm from the same artist”, which nothing could actually count.

    ⚠️ The Timekeeper reads #desk-drops DIRECTLY — deliberately not via JOB 4. JOB 4 owns the drops scan but is routinely skipped or part-run for budget, and the Overtime report runs overnight, ahead of any such pass. A signal parked behind a job that may not run arrives after the Review has already been filed, which defeats the whole point. This is a rare, intentional duplication of a read.

    Rulings encoded, so the same question is never asked twice: a staff no-arm drop is Case A with evidence — the T1 reminder is owed (the renter did forget), a charge never is (a staff arm records when staff armed, not when the renter left). Separately, from Melissa Lee 509B Aug 10: a disarm/arm pair under 5 minutes is not occupancy — she opened up, looked the room over, left, and came back at 7:25 for a 7:30 booking.     The Artist DB alarm code is a mirror, not a record — Doorman gets a daily job to keep it trueCHANGE

    Four artists were carrying a PIN that opens nothing, found in one afternoon: Shaka Lee, Garjanah Thayakumar, Nabil Fairbairn and Jai Smith. Every one of them is in the batch of five users created together (ids 247283561–247283571) whose Artist rows were left pointing at the codes of the deleted users they replaced. The 2026-08-09 reconciliation caught some of these and was hand-run; nothing has re-checked since, and drift resumed the same week.

    The Nabil case is the sharp one. His row was updated to 5523 on 08-10 and the SMS telling him so went out that night — but the code was never written to the panel, which still held 7492, the code he had disclosed to a non-renter the day before. So for four days the record said rotated, the renter had been told rotated, and the compromised PIN was the only one that worked. Written to the panel today and read back verified.

    New rule, in canon doorman-access-lifecycle.md: Alarm.com is the sole source of truth for a code; the Artist DB Alarm Code field exists only because the HTA message quotes it, so it is a mirror and the panel always wins. Job D added to the daily sweep — mirror every row carrying a code, once a day, plus a narrow pass immediately before any HTA batch so a message cannot quote yesterday's value. It refuses to mirror (and raises for a human) on split provisioning, on a missing panel user, and on a row that drifts again after being mirrored, which is a revert fight rather than a sync gap.

    Read gotcha, now fixed at the source: find_user_codes returns both code and code_slots, and after a panel-side change they disagree — code is a cached mirror that lags while the slot table is fresh. Garjanah's revert to 6440 read as "code": "3271" with all four slots already on 6440; reading code alone reported a successful staff change as failed, and a mirror built on it would have written the dead PIN into the exact field HTA sends. _record() in user_codes.py now derives effective_code from the slots so no caller has to know the rule.

    Plumbed the same night. The first design was a bulk code-read route returning every renter's PIN in one response; that was refused as a credential-dump surface on a public URL, and the narrower question turned out to be the one actually being asked. code_match instead takes the codes the caller already holds and answers match / differs / unknown — nothing new crosses the wire in either direction. Shipped as revision alarm-mcp-00017-8fr on both surfaces (MCP tool + POST /code-match), sharing one judge_claims() helper so they cannot drift into disagreeing verdicts. The Doorman prompt gained B(b4), one batched call per morning pass; first run is the ~06:00 Toronto fire.

    The trade, accepted deliberately: a differs cannot be self-healed, because fixing it would mean knowing the right value. Every one raises a Human-confirmed row pointing at the new attended audit-alarm-codes skill. Detection is cheap and daily; correction stays local and human.

    It found something on its first live run. Garjanah Thayakumar came back unknown / no panel user with this name — she had been deleted from Alarm.com hours after her code was mirrored into Notion, via a Processed job-C retirement row. Legitimate, but it left her row advertising a dead PIN. That produced the one carve-out in the rule: retirement is the single case where blanking a populated Alarm Code is correct — check for a Processed retirement row before deciding, because every other missing panel user is a provisioning bug whose evidence blanking would erase.

    First live actions — and the trip ceiling failed again, in the opposite directionFIX

    The watchdog took its first real actions. Studio 509B, 09:37–09:42 EDT: three disarm/re-arm cycles in six minutes on someone arriving, every one logging acted=True ok=True. The clearing worked. The stopping did not.

    Each cycle logged "Trip 1 of 2". The trip ceiling counts rising edges — the 08-14 fix for the opposite bug, where one five-minute siren counted as five trips. But an unbroken siren never produces a SECOND rising edge, so the count froze at 1, the ceiling became unreachable, and the watchdog would have cycled 509B indefinitely. Same twelve lines, same ceiling, failing both ways. Neither version was visible from the code: 08-14 needed a real long siren, 08-18 needed a real alarm that survived a re-arm.

    Fixed with a second, separate ceiling: two clear attempts against the alarm currently sounding, then suppress and call a human. Resets when the partition falls quiet; persists across restarts so a cold start mid-alarm cannot hand back a fresh allowance. An alarm that survives a clean re-arm is not the late-arrival case this was built for — something is re-triggering it, and repeatedly disarming a partition at a person is the wrong answer.

    New: a Panel Wi-Fi Connectivity Error now files its own Maintenance ticket (first one, 14:00:07Z). Deliberately a ticket and not a text — the link being down is real and worth fixing, but the panel keeps reporting over cellular, so coverage is not lost. Low battery remains the only condition that texts. Filed once per 24h against the same ledger the texts use; Credentials In Conflict and Sensor Bypassed are explicitly excluded, because ticketing constant noise is how a maintenance queue stops being read.

    The first deploy of that path 404'd — the Notion REST API wants the database id and got the data-source id the SQL tools hand you. It logged the failure, did not record it as filed, retried, and did not disturb the tick. That is the only reason it cost a redeploy instead of a silent gap.

    Watchdog ARMED for real — after the pre-flight found two ways it could have left a studio openCHANGE

    Revision alarm-mcp-00027-v9s, WATCHDOG_ARMED=true on Junyan's approval, first armed tick clean. It now auto-clears sirens and cycles stuck bypasses without a human.

    Two holes were found while checking the panel state immediately before arming, and 693 was sitting in exactly the state that triggers the first. (1) bypass_actions never checked faulted — a faulted partition can REFUSE a clean re-arm, so cycling it would disarm the studio and then fail to arm it back. 693 was faulted with an active AC Power Failure at that moment. (2) executor._cycle treated an accepted arm command as a successful one; an unverified re-arm now raises loudly and names the studio as possibly unprotected. Reporting ok=True on a studio left disarmed is the worst thing this system can do, because nobody goes looking.

    Both are the same lesson in different clothes: a command being accepted is not the world having changed. That is the third time on this project — first the bypass read-back, then the arm verification, now this.

    Kill switch unchanged: gcloud run services update alarm-mcp --region us-central1 --remove-env-vars WATCHDOG_ARMED.

    The alarm panel can now text a human — but only for the one condition that will not fix itselfNEW

When both are present the message says so, because a battery warning during a power cut is a countdown, not a nag.

Alerts are keyed on the panel's own wording, re-sent only after 6h, forgotten when the condition clears so a recurrence alerts again, and the alert state rides in the same GCS object as the ledgers so a cold start does not re-text everything open.

    Runs on every tick regardless of WATCHDOG_ARMED, deliberately. This is not the watchdog acting on a panel, it is telling a person something no automation can fix — gating it behind the arm switch would silence a dying panel exactly when the watchdog is paused.

    Why here and not on the Doorman: the Doorman already files the Urgent Issue row (it filed today's 693 pair at 03:07Z) but has no phone.com connector, so it cannot text; and its next pass can be up to 22 hours away.     What it will now do, unprompted: on a siren, wait 90s, re-read to confirm the trip is still real, then disarm that one partition by exact id and re-arm it to the mode it was in. On a bypassed sensor whose partition is armed, cycle that partition to bring the zone back into protection. Ceiling of 2 separate trips per partition per hour, then it stops and a human is called.   • 2026-08-14 · 19:30Z

    A real 509A trip proved the alarm signal — and exposed two bugs the dry run would have shippedFIX

    Studio 509A tripped for real at 15:20 UTC (11:20 EDT — the arrival on the 509 "Opened 11:19" log line), and the watchdog's dry run caught the whole thing. in_alarm does go true on a genuine trip, and faulted did not fire spuriously alongside it. That was the question the entire design rested on, and production answered it for free — the planned deliberate 693 trip is now off the list.

    Bug 1 — the rate ceiling counted ticks, not trips. The log read Trip 1 of 2, Trip 2 of 2, then 3 trips, 4 trips, 5 trips on consecutive minutes. That was one arrival with the siren running about five minutes. The rule Junyan set was "two trips in an hour, then call a human"; what was implemented was "any alarm lasting over two minutes calls a human" — true of every real alarm, so the ceiling meant nothing. TripLedger now counts rising edges, observing every partition every tick (the quiet readings are what make the next trip new). Armed, the route's _busy guard would have partly masked this — worse than an obvious failure, since it would have stayed invisible until the first clear that failed and then latched suppression on within two minutes.

    Bug 2 — 693's front door could never have been fixed. Its sensor is named simply Front Door, with no studio in the string, so the name-based sensor→partition matcher returned "ambiguous" and fell through to report-only. The watchdog would have reported that bypass forever without ever clearing it — on the one studio whose sensitive door is the reason this project exists. Caught live at 19:22 with 693 armed away and its front door bypassed. Matching now uses the panel id already inside the sensor's own id (101859741-1 and 101859741-127 are plainly one panel); names are still needed only to split the 509 panel's two partitions. The old tests passed because their fixtures used toy ids that shared no panel.

    Also settled: a clean re-arm does clear a bypass. 509A's cycle went out ~19:13, the 19:15 sweep still showed bypassed, and by 19:22 it was gone — the same shape as the 901 case, confirming that retraction and putting a number on the settle delay: minutes, not seconds.

    Durable ledgers landed (GCS, not Firestore — the Firestore API is off on this project and enabling it forces a permanent mode + location choice for two counters). Rising-edge counting only works if the quiet readings survive a restart, so the store became a prerequisite rather than a nicety. The load-bearing rule: empty state reads as "nothing has tripped, clear away", so a store that cannot be read must never hand empty state to an armed watchdog — a failed read fails the tick, and arming without a bucket is refused at startup.

  • 2026-08-14 · 19:00Z

    The alarm watchdog can now act — executor built, but it stays in dry runCHANGE

    Until now /watchdog decided correctly and did nothing: with WATCHDOG_ARMED set it returned 501 by design, because the half that sends commands did not exist. It exists now (alarm_monitor/executor.py, 15 tests against a fake bridge — there is no way to exercise this on the real panel without sirening occupied studios). The armed path disarms and cleanly re-arms one partition by exact id, runs on the same shared session that took the snapshot, and returns 207 when some actions fail and others succeed.

    The decision/execution split is kept absolute: watchdog.py still performs no I/O, so the dry run runs the identical decision code and simply never calls the executor. That is the only reason a dry-run log can be read as a preview of what would have happened.

    Three rules are written into it, each one paid for. An auto-clear re-reads before acting — it waits out the 90-second siren first, and in that time a person may well have disarmed the panel themselves. A bypass restore records the cycle as attempted and refuses to judge whether it worked: verify=True polls the partition state only and says nothing about the bypass flag, so the next scheduled pass reads fresh and decides — the 901 lesson moved from a comment into code. And arming never passes force_bypass, which would re-bypass the very sensor the cycle exists to protect and log a success for it.

    Nothing is armed. WATCHDOG_ARMED is still unset, and two prerequisites stand: the deliberate 693 trip, and durable ledgers. Both the trip count and the bypass-attempt count are in-memory, and Cloud Run recycles instances freely — a cold start would re-enable auto-clearing on a partition that had already earned a human.

  • 2026-08-14 · 14:00Z

    Reply approval moves into Notion — held queue rows must now carry their own thread contextCHANGE

    Junyan found the Claude-artifact conversation board too clunky to approve from, so the approval surface is now native Notion: 🕊️ Reply Approvals (page 3bc75032-81c4-8180-a65c-c76b2323e698, linked from the Staff Console) — a Pending-Review view of the 📤 Message Queue (oldest first) plus an unprocessed-inbound view. Approve = edit Message Body in place if needed, flip Status → Ready to Send; reject = Will Not Send. No new send path — this rides the existing release mechanic.

    The binding change is on the writers, not the readers (canon dc-canon 79cd3be0, JOB 2 + JOB 35): every row held at Pending Review must carry, in its page body, the inbound message it answers (or "start of thread"), the last up-to-10 exchanges from the 📬 Correspondence Log, and a why — so the row stands alone and the reviewer never has to open Gmail, WhatsApp, or a board. A held row with an empty body is not done. Existing rows are not backfilled (queue was empty at cutover). The desk-messages artifact stays published but is no longer the approval surface.
